> AGENT

There are **12 open megaplan tickets** in this repo.

I’d group them into four buckets:

**1. Big Architecture Decisions**
These should be decided first because they shape several implementation tickets.

- `01KRF1AY...` Should `pack` exist at all, or should Astrid move to a flat orchestrator registry?
- `01KRF156...` Decide whether packs are repo-internal modules or installable plugins.
- `01KRF17F...` Pack taxonomy is incoherent across builtin / external / upload / iteration / seinfeld / local.

Description: this is the “what is a pack?” cluster. It is mostly product architecture and naming/lifecycle design, not code cleanup yet.

**2. Pack Discovery / Manifest Correctness**
These are concrete problems in the current pack system.

- `01KRF15V...` `pack.yaml` schema is too weak to justify the manifest abstraction.
- `01KRF16M...` Custom flat YAML parser is a footgun.
- `01KRF16M...` `rglob` discovery can accidentally expose fixtures or half-built tools.
- `01KRF17G...` `discover_packs` silently skips directories without manifests.

Description: this is the “make pack discovery explicit and reliable” cluster. It depends on the big architecture choice: if packs become plugins, strengthen manifests; if packs stay internal, simplify them.

**3. Orchestrator Runtime / Authoring Path**
These are about duplicated or conflicting ways to define and run orchestrators.

- `01KRF15V...` Two orchestrator resolution paths produce divergent behavior for the same id.
- `01KRF17G...` `hype.py` vs `hype/` collision has no deprecation path.
- `01KRF16M...` `plan_template.py` is copy-pasted across three orchestrators.
- `01KRF1AZ...` Authoring a new orchestrator is high-ceremony and lacks scaffolding/docs.

Description: this is the “make orchestrators boring to create and resolve” cluster. The resolver divergence and `hype.py` collision should come before docs/scaffolding, because they determine the canonical pattern.

**4. Sandboxed Execution**
This one is mostly independent.

- `01KREY8...` Optional sandboxed run mode using devcontainer / Docker wrapper.

Description: this is a security and distribution feature. It touches developer UX and runtime isolation, but it does not need to wait on the pack/orchestrator architecture unless the sandbox is intended to support third-party plugin packs.

My recommended epic breakdown would be:

1. **Decide the Pack Model**: resolve pack existence, modules-vs-plugins, taxonomy.
2. **Unify Orchestrator Resolution**: pick one resolver, retire or move `hype.py`, preserve tests.
3. **Harden Pack Discovery and Manifests**: YAML/parser, schema, rglob boundaries, skipped-dir warnings.
4. **Improve Orchestrator Authoring UX**: shared plan builder, docs, scaffold command.
5. **Add Optional Sandboxed Runtime**: separate security/DevX epic.

Hey, so I wanted to basically be... So that someone can upload like a Giddup repo where they keep all their stuff related to a particular project and then upload that. Then that gets passed as components to different things. So that's kind of what I was thinking. and that we kind of somehow enforce the schema or make sure that they, you know, hold the overall thing and all the components of it so the elements, the orchestrators, the executors all follow like a particular schema or have some kind of like YAML thing to help people understand that and that all this is very clear to the agents who are using it so they kind of know which to use for what. And yeah, and then it's very easy to understand the instructions from a builder perspective and from a user perspective that their agent is used to know it's very easy for them to understand it too.

> AGENT

Yes. That points pretty clearly toward **“packs as installable project bundles/plugins”**, not “packs are just repo folders.”

I’d describe the desired model like this:

**Pack = uploadable project capability bundle**

A user can have a GitHub repo that contains everything related to one project/domain:

- executors: concrete actions the agent can run
- orchestrators: workflows that coordinate those actions
- elements: reusable visual/render components
- docs/instructions: what the pack is for, when to use it, examples
- schemas/manifests: machine-readable contracts for validation and agent discovery

That means the pack abstraction is real and worth keeping. It is not just internal organization.

The open tickets then collapse into a clearer product direction:

**1. Keep packs, make them real plugins**
Resolve the “do packs exist?” and “repo modules vs plugins?” tickets in favor of:

> Packs are installable Git-backed bundles with a declared schema, version, capabilities, and tool surface.

So a pack repo might look like:

```text
my-project-pack/
  pack.yaml
  README.md
  AGENTS.md
  executors/
    ingest_assets/
      executor.yaml
      STAGE.md
      run.py
  orchestrators/
    make_trailer/
      orchestrator.yaml
      STAGE.md
      run.py
  elements/
    effects/
      project-title-card/
        element.yaml
        component.tsx
  schemas/
    brief.schema.json
    asset-manifest.schema.json
```

**2. Make `pack.yaml` the source of truth**
The current `pack.yaml` ticket becomes central. It should probably declare:

```yaml
id: my_project
name: My Project Pack
version: 0.1.0
astrid_version: ">=0.3.0"

description: Tools for making videos and assets for My Project.

content:
  executors: executors
  orchestrators: orchestrators
  elements: elements
  schemas: schemas

capabilities:
  - video-editing
  - image-generation
  - project-assets

provides:
  executors:
    - my_project.ingest_assets
  orchestrators:
    - my_project.make_trailer
  elements:
    - effects/project-title-card

agent_docs:
  user: README.md
  builder: AGENTS.md
```

That solves discovery, installability, and “how does the agent know what this is for?”

**3. Use real YAML + schema validation**
The custom flat YAML parser should go. If packs are user-authored, YAML needs to behave like YAML.

Then every manifest gets validated:

- `pack.yaml` against `pack.schema.json`
- `executor.yaml` against `executor.schema.json`
- `orchestrator.yaml` against `orchestrator.schema.json`
- `element.yaml` against `element.schema.json`

This gives users clear errors like:

> `orchestrators/make_trailer/orchestrator.yaml: missing required field metadata.runtime_entrypoint`

instead of vague parser failures.

**4. Stop loose `rglob` discovery**
For this model, discovery should not scan randomly. It should read `pack.yaml`, then only inspect declared roots.

That avoids stray fixtures becoming product surface and makes the upload/install process deterministic.

**5. Split docs by audience**
I think you want two explicit doc layers:

- `README.md`: for the human user/builder. What is this pack, what can it do, how do I use it?
- `AGENTS.md` or `pack.instructions.md`: for agents. When to use each orchestrator/executor/element, constraints, examples, gotchas.

Each individual tool still has `STAGE.md`, but `STAGE.md` is local operational truth for that one tool. The pack-level agent doc is the map.

**6. Add a pack install / validate flow**
The UX should probably be something like:

```bash
python3 -m astrid packs validate ./my-project-pack
python3 -m astrid packs install https://github.com/user/my-project-pack
python3 -m astrid packs list
python3 -m astrid orchestrators list --pack my_project
python3 -m astrid orchestrators inspect my_project.make_trailer
```

And for builders:

```bash
python3 -m astrid packs new my_project
python3 -m astrid executors new my_project.ingest_assets
python3 -m astrid orchestrators new my_project.make_trailer
```

So the authoring-UX ticket becomes important, but downstream of the pack schema decision.

My recommendation: turn the current ticket cluster into one epic called something like **“Git-backed installable packs”**. It should resolve the architecture tickets with this position:

> Astrid packs are first-class, schema-validated, installable Git-backed capability bundles intended to be readable by both humans and agents.

Then implement in this order:

1. Define `pack.yaml` / executor / orchestrator / element schemas.
2. Replace flat YAML parser with real YAML + validation.
3. Change discovery to declared content roots.
4. Unify orchestrator resolution around manifests.
5. Add `packs validate` and `packs install <git-url>`.
6. Add scaffolding/docs for pack builders.

> DEVELOPER

And what else will we have to implement here? Templates? Something else?

> AGENT

Yes: beyond schemas and install/validate, you’ll want a few supporting pieces so this feels like a real builder/user/agent system instead of just “some YAML files in a repo.”

**Core Things To Implement**

1. **Pack Schema**
`pack.yaml` becomes the root contract.

It should declare:

- pack id/name/version
- compatible Astrid version
- content roots
- provided executors/orchestrators/elements
- dependencies
- required env vars/secrets
- docs/instruction files
- tags/capabilities/search keywords
- optional example assets or fixtures

2. **Per-Tool Schemas**
You need explicit schemas for:

- `executor.yaml`
- `orchestrator.yaml`
- `element.yaml`
- maybe `theme.yaml` later, if themes become packable

These should validate required fields and give useful errors.

3. **Templates / Scaffolding**
Yes, definitely.

You probably want templates for:

```text
pack/
executor/
orchestrator/
element-effect/
element-animation/
element-transition/
theme/
```

CLI commands:

```bash
python3 -m astrid packs new my_pack
python3 -m astrid executors new my_pack.download_assets
python3 -m astrid orchestrators new my_pack.make_video
python3 -m astrid elements new effect my_pack.title-card
```

The generated files should include:

- manifest YAML
- `STAGE.md`
- minimal `run.py`
- test stub
- README stub
- agent-instructions stub

4. **Validation Command**
A pack author should be able to run:

```bash
python3 -m astrid packs validate ./my-pack
```

It should check:

- all manifests parse
- schemas pass
- ids match folder/pack namespace
- runtime entrypoints import
- declared files exist
- docs referenced by `pack.yaml` exist
- no undeclared executor/orchestrator manifests are floating around
- no duplicate ids across installed packs
- elements compile or at least typecheck if possible

5. **Install / Registry Layer**
You need a place for installed packs to live outside the Astrid repo.

Something like:

```text
~/.astrid/packs/
  my_pack/
  another_pack/
```

Commands:

```bash
python3 -m astrid packs install https://github.com/user/my-pack
python3 -m astrid packs uninstall my_pack
python3 -m astrid packs update my_pack
python3 -m astrid packs list
python3 -m astrid packs inspect my_pack
```

Later, you can support a registry/index, but Git URL install is enough for v1.

6. **Discovery Refactor**
Discovery should read from:

- built-in repo packs
- installed user packs
- maybe project-local packs, e.g. `./astrid-packs/`

And it should discover only declared content roots from `pack.yaml`, not random `rglob`.

7. **Runtime Resolution Refactor**
The current orchestrator resolution split needs to collapse into one manifest-based path.

Canonical flow should be:

```text
id -> installed pack -> manifest -> runtime file/module -> entrypoint
```

This matters a lot for external Git-backed packs.

8. **Agent-Facing Index**
This is separate from human docs.

Astrid should be able to emit a compact “agent tool map”:

```bash
python3 -m astrid packs agent-index
python3 -m astrid packs inspect my_pack --agent
```

Output should answer:

- what this pack is for
- when to use each orchestrator
- when to use each executor
- what inputs/outputs each expects
- what not to use it for
- examples

This is how the agent knows which component to pick.

9. **Builder Docs**
You’ll want a clear builder guide:

```text
docs/creating-packs.md
docs/pack-schema.md
docs/executor-schema.md
docs/orchestrator-schema.md
docs/element-schema.md
docs/examples/simple-pack/
```

And the template output should point back to those docs.

10. **Examples / Fixtures**
You need at least two complete example packs:

- **minimal-pack**: one executor, one orchestrator, one README
- **media-pack**: executor + orchestrator + element + schema + sample assets

These become both documentation and regression tests.

11. **Tests**
Important tests:

- valid pack passes validation
- malformed YAML gives clear errors
- undeclared manifests are rejected or warned
- installed pack tools show up in `list/search/inspect`
- duplicate ids are caught
- runtime entrypoint loads from installed pack
- agent index includes expected summaries
- templates generate a valid pack

12. **Dependency / Secret Model**
For uploaded packs, this matters.

A pack may need:

```yaml
dependencies:
  python:
    - requests>=2
  system:
    - ffmpeg
  npm:
    - some-package

secrets:
  - name: OPENAI_API_KEY
    required: true
  - name: RUNPOD_API_KEY
    required: false
```

You do not need automatic dependency installation on day one, but validation should at least report what the pack declares.

**I’d call the full feature:**

> Git-backed schema-validated Astrid packs

The likely implementation epics are:

1. **Pack Contract**: schemas, docs, examples.
2. **Pack Runtime**: discovery, install location, id resolution.
3. **Pack UX**: `packs new`, templates, `packs validate`, useful errors.
4. **Agent UX**: agent index, clearer inspect/search output.
5. **Migration**: convert existing built-in packs to the new layout gradually.

criteria:\n+\n+- There is one canonical runtime resolution path.\n+- `builtin.hype` no longer has two different public meanings.\n+- Installed-pack orchestrators can run through the standard CLI.\n+\n+### Phase 6: Builder Scaffolding\n+\n+Goal: make pack authoring easy and hard to get subtly wrong.\n+\n+Tasks:\n+\n+- Add templates for packs, executors, orchestrators, and elements.\n+- Add except ids/names.\n+\n+Exit criteria:\n+\n+- A builder can generate a minimal pack and pass `packs validate`.\n+- Template-generated orchestrators use the canonical runtime path.\n+- New authoring docs point to scaffolded examples, not historical files.\n+\n+### Phase 7: Agent Index And Documentation Polish\n+\n+Goal: make installed packs legible to agents and humans.\n+\n+Tasks:\n+\n+- Implement `packs agent-index`.\n+- Improve `inspect --json` output for pack components.\n+- Add guidance for writing pack-level `AGENTS.md`.\n+- Add a richer media example pack with executor, orchestrator, element, schema,\n+  and example assets.\n+- Add docs for secrets, dependencies, and when to use orchestrators vs\n+  executors.\n+\n+Exit criteria:\n+\n+- An agent can inspect installed packs and choose the right entrypoint.\n+- A human builder can follow docs from blank repo to validated pack.\n+\n+## Existing Ticket Mapping\n+\n+Architecture decisions:\n+\n+- `01KRF1AYT8ANAD47QB1M535SH4`: Keep packs as a real abstraction layer.\n+- `01KRF156A3M3WTDQVQGX797JAM`: Resolve in favor of installable plugins.\n+- `01KRF17FVN1C24HQ8TFJJNPW0G`: Define taxonomy around installable capability\n+  bundles and built-in platform packs.\n+\n+Pack manifests and discovery:\n+\n+- `01KRF15VTT59Q0P8N6R2619TRG`: Expand `pack.yaml` into a real contract.\n+- `01KRF16M8EEZF7475ATCG7GSJ6`: Replace the custom YAML parser.\n+- `01KRF16MJWRHGWEQZ2A8NTSFSY`: Replace loose `rglob` with declared roots.\n+- `01KRF17GHJN770ZDRYEKT9R3V0`: Warn on likely pack directories with missing\n+  manifests.\n+\n+Orchestrator authoring and runtime:\n+\n+- `01KRF15VFMCN7SYZ9K4NANXTAT`: Unify resolver behavior.\n+- `01KRF17G6NARVX8DYSKQCBY8NS`: Move, rename, or delete the legacy `hype.py`\n+  fixture.\n+- `01KRF16MX3VWY2Q993VHHAKNTG`: Extract a small shared plan builder.\n+- `01KRF1AZ4G2V8PKH3DX9FK81D2`: Add scaffolding and authoring docs.\n+\n+Independent security work:\n+\n+- `01KREY8RVFADZQK9X3GBBDXD03`: Optional sandboxed runtime. This complements\n+  installable packs but does not block the pack contract.\n+\n+## Open Questions\n+\n+- Should `provides` list every component manually, or should validation generate\n+  the inventory from declared roots?\n+- Should installed packs be copied, cloned, or referenced in place?\n+- Should Astrid support semver compatibility checks in v1 or only warn?\n+- Should pack dependencies be informational at first, or should install attempt\n+  to provision Python/npm/system dependencies?\n+- Should a pack be allowed to override built-in element ids, or must overrides\n+  live in an explicit project-local layer?\n+- Should the agent index be pure generated output, or should packs be allowed to\n+  ship a hand-authored concise agent summary?\n+\n+## Recommended First Epic\n+\n+Start with a single epic called:\n+\n+```text\n+Git-backed schema-validated Astrid packs\n+```\n+\n+The first implementation slice should be intentionally narrow:\n+\n+1. Add schemas.\n+2. Add `packs validate <path>`.\n+3. Add a minimal external example pack.\n+4. Replace the flat YAML parser on the validation path.\n+5. Make validation errors good enough that a builder can fix their repo without\n+   reading Astrid internals.\n+\n+Do not start with Git install. > DEVELOPER

COuld this all be done in aa single two week sprint?

> AGENT

No, not responsibly. A **thin validation-only slice** could fit in a single two-week sprint. The full plan is more like **3-5 focused sprints**, depending on how much polish you want.

For one two-week sprint, I’d scope it to:

**Sprint 1: Pack Contract + Validation**

Deliverables:

- Define `pack.yaml`, `executor.yaml`, `orchestrator.yaml`, `element.yaml` schemas.
- Replace/bypass the flat YAML parser for pack validation.
- Add `python3 -m astrid packs validate <path>`.
- Add one `examples/packs/minimal/` external-style pack.
- Validate built-in packs enough to avoid breaking current discovery.
- Produce clear, file-specific validation errors.
- Add docs for the target pack layout and first version of the manifest contract.

That is valuable on its own because it turns the idea into a real contract. It also de-risks every later step.

I would **not** include these in the same sprint:

- GitHub install/update/uninstall.
- `~/.astrid/packs` registry.
- Full discovery refactor across built-in + installed packs.
- Unified runtime resolution.
- Orchestrator scaffolding commands.
- Agent index generation.
- Dependency installation.
- Sandbox support.

Those are each small product surfaces with failure modes. Cramming them into two weeks would likely create a half-plugin system that agents can inspect but not reliably run, or run but not reliably validate.

A realistic sprint breakdown:

1. **Contract + Validation**: schemas, parser, validate CLI, example pack, docs.
2. **Discovery + Install**: installed pack root, Git install, explicit content roots, collision checks.
3. **Runtime Resolution**: manifest-based execution path, resolve `hype.py` collision, installed orchestrators runnable.
4. **Builder UX**: templates, scaffold commands, plan builder, authoring docs.
5. **Agent UX Polish**: agent index, better inspect/search, richer examples.

If you want a two-week sprint with a demo at the end, aim for:

> “Here is a Git-style pack folder. Astrid validates it, explains what is wrong, and can list/inspect its declared components locally.”

That’s the right first wedge.

> AGENT

Done. I ran the same four lenses through DeepSeek and compared them against the first four-reviewer panel.

**High-Confidence Consensus**

Both panels strongly agree on these changes:

1. **Validation-first sequencing is right.**
The core spine is sound: contract -> validation -> deterministic discovery -> install -> runtime.

2. **Manual `provides` should be removed.**
Both panels called it drift-prone. The plan should choose:
`declared roots + component manifests -> generated inventory`.

3. **Security/trust is too late.**
Sandbox implementation can wait, but the **security model cannot**. Before install/runtime, the plan needs trust tiers, explicit “packs are executable code” warnings, secret handling, install review, and pinned commits.

4. **Agent legibility needs to move earlier.**
The agent index cannot wait until Sprint 6 as “polish.” At minimum, Sprint 1-3 need structured fields for:
`purpose`, `normal entrypoints`, `do not use for`, `required context`, `secrets`, `dependencies`.

5. **Scaffolding comes too late.**
Builder lens and DeepSeek both said Sprint 5 is too late. Add a minimal `packs new <id>` in Sprint 1, even if full executor/orchestrator/element scaffolding stays later.

6. **Dependencies need a hard v1 stance.**
Declare them as informational only in v1, or create an environment model. Do not imply Astrid installs Python/npm/system deps unless that is intentionally designed.

7. **Runtime contract is underspecified.**
The plan needs to define what `run.py` exposes: CLI shape, callable shape, argv/env behavior, output conventions, exit codes, error wrapping.

**DeepSeek Added Useful Contrarian Points**

DeepSeek pushed harder on a few abstractions:

- **Global pack ids may need stronger namespacing.**
Bare ids like `my_project` may collide. Consider GitHub-style or reverse-domain ids, or at least make collision policy explicit.

- **`STAGE.md` may be too Astrid-internal as a public requirement.**
DeepSeek argued for a manifest `doc:` field or optional docs instead of requiring a magic filename for every component.

- **Simple packs may need a lower-ceremony “flat pack” path.**
This is worth considering carefully. It conflicts with strict manifest purity, but the builder pain is real.

- **Schema versioning needs to be explicit.**
Add something like `schema_version: 1` or `pack_schema: astrid.pack.v1`; do not rely only on `astrid_version`.

- **Git install should maybe start with local path install first.**
DeepSeek argued Git URL install brings auth, supply chain, pinning, private repo, and update complexity. That may be right: Sprint 3 could be `packs install ./path` first, then Git URL once pinning/trust is designed.

**Where Panels Disagreed**

- **Delete legacy runtime path vs deprecate it.**
Technical/architect reviewers want one resolver quickly. DeepSeek abstractions suggested a temporary adapter. I’d split the difference: new external packs must use manifest runtime; built-ins get one migration sprint with warnings, then legacy path is removed.

- **Structured agent contract vs `AGENTS.md`.**
DeepSeek distrusts Markdown for agent behavior. I think the better answer is: structured manifest fields are authoritative; `AGENTS.md` is supplemental prose.

- **Inline/flat components vs strict per-component manifests.**
DeepSeek builder lens wants inline components for low ceremony. I’d avoid adding that in v1 unless you’re prepared to support two authoring models. Instead, put `packs new` in Sprint 1 and make the template tiny.

**Recommended Document Changes**

I’d amend the plan with these concrete changes:

1. Add **Sprint 0: Architecture Decisions**
Trust tiers, security posture, version/update semantics, dependency model, namespace policy, migration policy.

2. Move minimal **`packs new <id>` into Sprint 1**.
It should generate a valid blank pack.

3. Remove manual `provides` from v1.
Use generated inventory.

4. Add **install trust model** before install:
`install --dry-run`, pinned commit, confirmation summary, lockfile, rollback/update semantics.

5. Add **per-component secrets**.
Pack-level secrets are definitions; components declare actual usage.

6. Add **schema versioning** to `pack.yaml`.

7. Add **runtime contract** details in Sprint 1 docs.

8. Move minimal agent summary fields into the manifest contract early.

9. Add a **PackResolver** abstraction to avoid raw filesystem/source-tree assumptions.

10. Make dependency provisioning explicitly out of scope for v1, with `packs doctor` later for readiness checks.

Net: the plan is directionally approved by both panels, but it needs security/trust, versioning, generated inventory, and minimal scaffolding pulled forward before install/runtime work.

> DEVELOPER

who gave better feedback, deepseek or gpt 5.5

> AGENT

GPT-5.5 gave the better overall feedback.

It was more grounded in the actual Astrid plan and current repo shape: `provides` drift, `PackResolver`, runtime contract, element/Remotion implications, `hype.py`, install activation, per-component secrets, `packs doctor`, commit pinning, and moving agent inspect earlier. Those are directly actionable against the document.

DeepSeek was useful, especially on security, supply chain, schema versioning, and builder ceremony. But it also pushed a few ideas I’d treat cautiously:

- reverse-domain global ids may be overkill for Astrid v1
- arbitrary component types could blur useful Astrid concepts
- replacing `STAGE.md` entirely may fight existing project conventions
- inline/flat-pack components add a second authoring model too early
- “never query raw Markdown” is probably too rigid; better to make structured fields authoritative and Markdown supplemental

So my ranking:

1. **GPT-5.5 panel**: better product/architecture fit, fewer distractions, stronger implementation sequencing.
2. **DeepSeek panel**: strong adversarial security/supply-chain pressure, but more likely to over-generalize.

The best synthesis is GPT-5.5 as the main corrective path, with DeepSeek’s security/versioning warnings promoted into the plan.

The lower-maintenance v1 is probably declared roots plus generated\n-component inventory.\n+`provides` should not be manually authored in v1. Declared content roots and\n+component manifests define the public search space; validation generates the\n+component inventory for `inspect`, `agent-index`, and install summaries. Manual\n+inventories are too likely to drift.\n+\n+Dependencies are declarative in v1. Astrid validates their shape and surfaces\n+them in `inspect`, install summaries, and doctor checks, but it does not\n+automatically install Python, npm, or system dependencies until an explicit\n+environment model exists.\n@@\n The schemas should define required fields, allowed values, runtime entrypoints,\n input/output declarations, docs references, and searchable metadata.\n \n Manifests should be written for both machines and people. A builder should be\n able to read one and understand what the component does, and an agent should be\n able to inspect one and decide whether the component fits a task.\n+\n+The structured manifest fields are authoritative for agent behavior. Markdown\n+docs such as `README.md`, `AGENTS.md`, and `STAGE.md` are supplemental prose.\n+They can explain nuance and examples, but they should not be the only source for\n+normal entrypoints, required inputs, secrets, or \"do not use for\" guidance.\n+\n+External manifest runtime fields should describe stable adapter semantics, not\n+current internal import mechanics. Prefer fields like:\n+\n+```yaml\n+runtime:\n+  type: python-cli\n+  entrypoint: run.py\n+  callable: main\n+```\n+\n+over internal names such as `runtime_module`.\n@@\n Runtime resolution should become manifest-based and singular.\n \n Canonical flow:\n@@\n   -> entrypoint\n ```\n \n+Introduce a `PackResolver` abstraction as part of this work. It should resolve\n+pack roots, component roots, docs, schemas, runtime files, examples, assets, and\n+installed revisions. Runtime resolution, validation, inspect, and agent-index\n+should consume this resolver instead of assuming components live under the\n+source tree.\n+\n This replaces the current split between:\n@@\n The `builtin.hype` folder should become the canonical pattern. The legacy\n `hype.py` DSL fixture should move to tests, be renamed as a fixture, or be\n deleted after resolver unification.\n+\n+## Review Synthesis\n+\n+Two independent review panels agreed on the core direction and found the same\n+high-risk gaps.\n+\n+High-confidence changes:\n+\n+- Validation-first sequencing is correct.\n+- Manual `provides` should be removed in favor of generated inventory.\n+- Security, trust, versioning, and update semantics must be designed before\n+  install/runtime, even if full sandboxing ships later.\n+- Minimal scaffolding should arrive early; a blank-repo builder needs\n+  `packs new <id>` in the first implementation sprint.\n+- Agent-legible structured fields should move into the manifest contract early,\n+  not wait for late-stage polish.\n+- Dependencies should be declaration-only in v1 unless an environment model is\n+  deliberately implemented.\n+- Runtime contracts need to define callable shape, argv/env behavior, output\n+  conventions, exit codes, and error wrapping.\n+\n+Useful cautions:\n+\n+- Keep structured fields authoritative and Markdown supplemental.\n+- Consider stronger namespace policy before a public ecosystem grows; bare pack\n+  ids are acceptable for v1 only if collisions are detected and rejected.\n+- Treat external elements as a harder problem than executors/orchestrators\n+  because Remotion registry and compilation paths are source-tree-sensitive.\n+- Avoid adding a second \"inline component\" authoring model too early. Lower the\n+  ceremony with scaffolding and good templates first.\n \n ## Two-Week Sprint Roadmap\n \n This project should be sequenced as several two-week sprints. The right order is\n-contract first, then discovery, then installation, then execution, then authoring\n-and agent polish. Installing GitHub repos before the validation contract exists\n-would produce a brittle plugin surface; making runtime resolution support\n-external packs before discovery is deterministic would make the behavior hard to\n-reason about.\n+architecture decisions, contract, validation, discovery, local install, Git\n+install, execution, authoring, and agent polish. Installing GitHub repos before\n+the validation/trust contract exists would produce a brittle plugin surface;\n+making runtime resolution support external packs before discovery is\n+deterministic would make the behavior hard to reason about.\n+\n+### Sprint 0: Architecture Decisions\n+\n+Goal: settle the constraints that would be expensive to change after install and\n+runtime ship.\n+\n+Deliverables:\n+\n+- Define trust tiers: built-in, local workspace pack, installed third-party\n+  pack, and future curated pack.\n+- Define security posture for install and run: validation is not a sandbox,\n+  packs are executable code, and secrets are only passed to components that\n+  declare them.\n+- Define version and update semantics: schema version, `astrid_version`\n+  compatibility, install records, pinned revisions, and rollback expectations.\n+- Define namespace policy for pack ids and component ids, including collision\n+  handling and whether stronger namespacing is required before public registry\n+  use.\n+- Define dependency policy: declarations are informational/diagnostic in v1;\n+  no automatic Python/npm/system provisioning.\n+- Define migration policy for built-in legacy resolver paths.\n+\n+Explicitly out of scope:\n+\n+- Implementing sandbox runtime.\n+- Implementing install/update.\n+- Migrating every built-in pack.\n+\n+Exit criteria:\n+\n+- The plan has crisp answers for trust, security, versioning, dependencies,\n+  namespace collisions, and migration.\n+- Later sprints do not need to guess how install or runtime should behave.\n \n ### Sprint 1: Pack Contract And Validation\n \n Goal: establish the external pack contract before changing runtime behavior.\n@@\n - Decide that packs are installable Git-backed capability bundles.\n - Decide that packs remain the top-level grouping and namespace layer.\n - Write initial schemas for `pack.yaml`, `executor.yaml`, `orchestrator.yaml`,\n   and `element.yaml`.\n+- Add `schema_version` to pack manifests and reject unknown schema versions.\n+- Add structured agent fields for purpose, normal entrypoints, \"do not use\n+  for\", required context, secrets, and dependencies.\n+- Define the runtime contract in docs: callable shape, argv/env behavior, output\n+  conventions, exit codes, and error wrapping.\n - Replace or bypass the flat YAML parser on the validation path with a real YAML\n   parser.\n - Implement `python3 -m astrid packs validate <path>`.\n+- Implement minimal `python3 -m astrid packs new <id>` that generates a valid\n+  pack skeleton.\n - Add one minimal external-style example pack under `examples/packs/minimal/`.\n - Add `docs/creating-packs.md` and first-pass schema reference docs.\n - Add tests for valid and invalid example packs.\n@@\n - Git install/update/uninstall.\n - Installed pack discovery.\n - Runtime execution from external packs.\n-- Scaffolding commands.\n+- Full executor/orchestrator/element scaffolding commands.\n - Dependency installation.\n@@\n - A valid example pack passes validation.\n+- `packs new <id>` produces a skeleton that passes validation.\n - Broken example packs fail with clear, file-specific errors.\n - Existing built-in manifests still parse or are explicitly documented as\n   legacy-compatible.\n@@\n-### Sprint 3: Local And Git-Installed Packs\n+### Sprint 3: Local Pack Install And Trust UX\n \n-Goal: let an external repository become an installed Astrid pack.\n+Goal: let a local external pack become installed without opening the Git supply\n+chain surface yet.\n \n Deliverables:\n \n - Add installed-pack root, probably `~/.astrid/packs/`.\n-- Implement `packs install <git-url-or-path>`.\n+- Implement `packs install <local-path>`.\n+- Implement `packs install --dry-run <local-path>` with a trust summary.\n - Implement `packs list`, `packs inspect`, `packs update`, and\n   `packs uninstall`.\n+- Write an install record or lockfile with pack id, source path, installed\n+  revision marker, pack version, schema version, Astrid compatibility, and\n+  install time.\n - Validate packs before completing installation.\n+- Use activation language: copy/clone, validate, then activate. Failed\n+  validation leaves no active pack.\n - Include installed packs in executor, orchestrator, and element list/search/\n   inspect commands.\n - Reject or clearly report id collisions with built-in and installed packs.\n+- Add a basic `packs inspect <id> --agent` summary using structured manifest\n+  fields.\n \n Explicitly out of scope:\n \n+- Git URL install.\n - Running installed-pack orchestrators if runtime resolution still depends on\n   source-tree-only imports.\n - Automatic dependency provisioning.\n - Pack registry or marketplace.\n@@\n-- A valid external Git repo or local path can be installed.\n+- A valid local external pack can be installed.\n - Invalid packs are rejected before installation completes.\n - Installed pack components appear in list/search/inspect output.\n - Uninstall removes the pack cleanly from discovery.\n+- Install summaries clearly show tools, executable entrypoints, secrets,\n+  dependencies, docs, and warnings.\n+\n+### Sprint 4: Git Install, Pinning, And Update Safety\n+\n+Goal: support Git-backed packs without making arbitrary branch tips silently\n+executable.\n+\n+Deliverables:\n+\n+- Implement `packs install <git-url-or-path>` for Git URLs.\n+- Resolve and record a concrete commit SHA for every Git install.\n+- Show a confirmation summary before activation unless `--yes` is passed.\n+- Revalidate before activation.\n+- Implement `packs update --dry-run` with manifest diff summary.\n+- Keep the previous installed revision and support rollback.\n+- Define private-repo behavior: use the user's existing Git credentials via the\n+  Git subprocess; do not manage GitHub tokens directly in v1.\n+\n+Explicitly out of scope:\n+\n+- Marketplace or curated registry.\n+- Signature verification.\n+- Automatic dependency provisioning.\n+\n+Exit criteria:\n+\n+- Git installs are pinned, inspectable, and reversible.\n+- Updates cannot silently swap executable code without validation and summary.\n+- The install record is sufficient to reproduce which pack revision was active.\n \n-### Sprint 4: Unified Runtime Resolution\n+### Sprint 5: Unified Runtime Resolution\n \n Goal: make installed pack components runnable through the same path as built-in\n components.\n@@\n - Collapse orchestrator resolution to the manifest-based path.\n+- Introduce and use `PackResolver` for installed and built-in pack resources.\n - Preserve current CLI and runner behavior for existing built-in orchestrators.\n - Move, rename, or delete the legacy `builtin/hype.py` DSL fixture.\n - Define whether the DSL decorator remains supported or becomes legacy.\n - Add tests proving the same id resolves the same way from all call sites.\n@@\n - There is one canonical runtime resolution path.\n - `builtin.hype` no longer has two different public meanings.\n - Installed-pack orchestrators can run through the standard CLI.\n \n-### Sprint 5: Builder Scaffolding And Templates\n+### Sprint 6: Builder Scaffolding And Templates\n \n Goal: make pack authoring easy and hard to get subtly wrong.\n \n Deliverables:\n \n-- Add templates for packs, executors, orchestrators, and elements.\n-- Add `packs new`, `executors new`, `orchestrators new`, and `elements new`.\n+- Expand templates for packs, executors, orchestrators, and elements.\n+- Add `executors new`, `orchestrators new`, and `elements new`.\n - Extract a small plan-v2 builder for orchestrator plan templates.\n - Ensure generated templates validate without manual edits except ids/names.\n - Update authoring docs to point at scaffolded examples instead of historical\n@@\n - Template-generated orchestrators use the canonical runtime path.\n - The canonical examples no longer require copying production pack internals.\n \n-### Sprint 6: Agent Index And Pack Legibility\n+### Sprint 7: Agent Index And Pack Legibility\n \n Goal: make installed packs easy for agents and humans to understand.\n@@\n - An agent can inspect installed packs and choose the right entrypoint.\n - The index explains normal entrypoints, low-level building blocks, required\n   secrets, and important constraints.\n - Human docs and agent docs have clear separate roles.\n \n-### Sprint 7: Rich Example Pack And Hardening\n+### Sprint 8: Rich Example Pack And Hardening\n \n Goal: prove the system on a realistic media pack and fix the rough edges found\n by using it.\n@@\n - The docs can guide a builder from blank repo to runnable installed pack.\n - The remaining limitations are explicit.\n \n-### Optional Sprint 8: Sandboxed Pack Runtime\n+### Sprint 9: Legacy Migration\n+\n+Goal: prevent a permanent two-world system where built-ins keep historical\n+rules while external packs follow stricter contracts.\n+\n+Deliverables:\n+\n+- Convert one real built-in pack fully to the external pack contract.\n+- Document compatibility gaps found during migration.\n+- Add deprecation warnings for legacy resolver paths.\n+- Decide whether remaining built-ins migrate immediately or through an alias/\n+  compatibility window.\n+- Update tests so the converted built-in pack exercises the same resolver and\n+  validation code as external packs.\n+\n+Explicitly out of scope:\n+\n+- Renaming every pack or changing public ids without aliases.\n+- Marketplace work.\n+\n+Exit criteria:\n+\n+- At least one real built-in pack proves the external contract is not only for\n+  fixtures.\n+- Legacy resolver behavior has a clear end state.\n+\n+### Optional Sprint 10: Sandboxed Pack Runtime\n \n Goal: provide an opt-in security boundary for running user-installed packs.\n@@\n ```text\n-contract -> validation -> deterministic discovery -> install -> runtime ->\n-scaffolding -> agent index -> realistic examples -> sandbox\n+architecture decisions -> contract -> validation -> deterministic discovery ->\n+local install -> Git install -> runtime -> scaffolding -> agent index ->\n+realistic examples -> migration -> sandbox\n ```\n \n Key reasons:\n \n - Validation must come before install, otherwise Astrid can install ambiguous or\n   malformed repositories.\n+- Trust, versioning, and dependency policy must come before install, otherwise\n+  unsafe defaults harden into product behavior.\n - Declared discovery must come before runtime execution, otherwise stray\n   manifests can become runnable product surface.\n-- Runtime unification must come before scaffolding, otherwise templates may\n-  teach the wrong authoring pattern.\n+- Local install should precede Git install so the activation and discovery model\n+  can stabilize before handling authentication, pinning, and updates.\n+- Runtime unification must come before full component scaffolding, otherwise\n+  templates may teach the wrong authoring pattern.\n - Scaffolding must come before broad docs polish, otherwise docs will describe\n   manual copy-paste rather than the intended builder flow.\n - Agent index should come after manifests and inspect output stabilize.\n - Sandbox mode is valuable, but it should wrap a coherent pack system rather\n@@\n - Should `provides` list every component manually, or should validation generate\n   the inventory from declared roots?\n - Should installed packs be copied, cloned, or referenced in place?\n-- Should Astrid support semver compatibility checks in v1 or only warn?\n-- Should pack dependencies be informational at first, or should install attempt\n-  to provision Python/npm/system dependencies?\n+- Should pack ids remain simple strings for v1, or should public packs require\n+  stronger global namespacing?\n - Should a pack be allowed to override built-in element ids, or must overrides\n   live in an explicit project-local layer?\n-- Should the agent index be pure generated output, or should packs be allowed to\n-  ship a hand-authored concise agent summary?\n+- How much of element support should ship in v1, given Remotion registry and\n+  compilation assumptions?\n+- Should `STAGE.md` remain mandatory for external components, or should a\n+  manifest `docs` field be enough for simple executors?\n@@\n The first implementation slice should be intentionally narrow:\n \n 1. Add schemas.\n 2. Add `packs validate <path>`.\n-3. Add a minimal external example pack.\n-4. Replace the flat YAML parser on the validation path.\n-5. Make validation errors good enough that a builder can fix their repo without\n+3. Add `packs new <id>`.\n+4. Add a minimal external example pack.\n+5. Replace the flat YAML parser on the validation path.\n+6. Make validation errors good enough that a builder can fix their repo without\n    reading Astrid internals.\n \n Do not start with Git install. Validation is the foundation; install should only\n accept packs whose contract is already clear.\n*** End Patch\n"

> DEVELOPER

consider $go-all-the-way and see if it influences any decisions or dierction

> AGENT

Yes. Interpreted as a product principle, it argues against a halfway plugin system.

The plan should bias toward **fewer, more complete slices** rather than many thin surfaces that look real but do not fully work. Concretely:

- Do not ship Git install until local installed packs can actually **validate -> install -> inspect -> run**.
- Do not expose “pack dependencies” unless the UX clearly says whether Astrid manages them or only reports them.
- Do not make agent legibility optional polish. If agents are a core user, structured agent-facing metadata belongs in the v1 contract.
- Do not support every component type equally on day one if elements/Remotion are much harder. It is better to fully support executors/orchestrators first than half-support executors/orchestrators/elements.
- Do not keep dual resolver paths lingering. If manifest runtime is the future, prove it early and migrate real built-ins.

So the directional influence is: **commit harder to the coherent product shape, but narrow v1 enough that each shipped layer is real.**

The sprint plan already moved in that direction after the sequencing review. The one additional change I’d consider is making the first public milestone explicitly:

> “External executor/orchestrator packs are fully usable. External elements are schema-valid and inspectable, but render integration lands in the hardening sprint.”

That is more “all the way” than pretending all pack types are equally ready.

> DEVELOPER

but is going all the way not arguing towards completing features?

> AGENT

Yes. It argues for completing features, but not for making the first feature set huge.

The distinction is:

- Bad: ship 30% of install, 30% of runtime, 30% of elements, 30% of agent UX.
- Good: ship 100% of a narrower capability.

So for this project, “go all the way” should mean the v1 milestone is not “schemas exist” or “Git repos can be installed but not run.” It should be a complete vertical slice:

> A builder can create an executor/orchestrator pack, validate it, install it locally, inspect it, and run it. A user can understand what it does, what secrets/dependencies it needs, and uninstall it cleanly.

That is a completed feature.

Then Git install is another completed feature:

> Install from Git at a pinned commit, see trust summary, validate before activation, inspect, run, update safely, and rollback.

External elements are probably another completed feature, not bundled halfway into v1:

> Install a pack with elements, generate registry/type artifacts, render a timeline using the element, and debug failures clearly.

So yes: going all the way argues for **vertical completeness per milestone**, not breadth. It should push the plan away from “phase 1 is just documents and schemas” if that phase is meant to be user-facing. As internal groundwork, fine. As a shipped feature, not enough.

> DEVELOPER

So what would you recommend?

> AGENT

I’d recommend reframing the roadmap around **completed vertical milestones**, not infrastructure phases.

Keep the same technical sequencing internally, but define the first real milestone as:

**Milestone 1: Local Executor/Orchestrator Packs Work End To End**

Ship when all of this is true:

- `packs new <id>` creates a valid pack.
- The pack can contain at least one executor and one orchestrator.
- `packs validate <path>` gives useful errors.
- `packs install <local-path>` activates it.
- `packs inspect <id>` and `packs inspect <id> --agent` explain it.
- The installed executor/orchestrator can run through the normal CLI.
- Secrets/dependencies are shown clearly, even if not auto-managed.
- `packs uninstall <id>` removes it cleanly.
- One real built-in pack has been migrated or exercised through the same path.

That is a complete feature. It may take 2-3 two-week sprints, but it is worth treating as one milestone.

Then:

**Milestone 2: Git-Backed Packs Work End To End**

- Install from Git URL.
- Pin commit.
- Show trust summary.
- Validate before activation.
- Run installed components.
- Update with dry-run/diff.
- Rollback.
- Private repos use existing Git credentials.

**Milestone 3: Builder UX Is First-Class**

- `executors new`
- `orchestrators new`
- richer templates
- docs from blank repo to working pack
- plan builder extraction
- golden-path examples

**Milestone 4: Agent UX Is First-Class**

- full `packs agent-index`
- better inspect JSON
- clear entrypoint recommendations
- “do not use for” semantics
- examples agents can copy

**Milestone 5: External Elements Work End To End**

- element pack validation
- registry generation
- type generation
- render path
- realistic media pack
- Remotion failure/debug story

**Milestone 6: Trust/Sandbox Hardening**

- optional sandbox mode
- tighter secret handling
- dependency doctor
- curated/verified pack story if needed

So I would edit the doc to say:

> Sprints are implementation slices, but releases should happen only at vertical milestones. Do not present an infrastructure slice as a completed user-facing feature.

This preserves momentum while honoring “go all the way.”

> AGENT

Rubric take: do **not** run [docs/git-backed-packs-plan.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/Astrid/docs/git-backed-packs-plan.md) as one megaplan. It is correctly scoped as a roadmap made of separate two-week megaplan-sized sprints.

Recommended profile map:

| Scope | Megaplan Profile | Why |
|---|---:|---|
| Sprint 0 architecture gate | `super-premium/robust/high` | This freezes trust, namespace, versioning, install semantics, and schema direction. Downstream sprints depend on it. |
| Sprint 1 contract + validation | `super-premium/robust/high` if schema is being frozen; otherwise `premium/robust/high` | Public manifest/schema contract is foundational. If Sprint 0 resolves enough decisions, downgrade to `premium`. |
| Sprint 2 PackResolver + runtime unification | `premium//high` | Cross-cutting runtime path touching discovery, inspect, and execution. Needs strong repo reasoning, but not necessarily robust process. |
| Sprint 3 local install + runnable packs | `premium//medium` | Activation/install state is important, but if Sprint 2 landed cleanly the shape is mostly implementation. |
| Sprint 4 Git install + pin/update/rollback | `premium/robust/high` | Supply-chain surface. Pinning, rollback, and trust summaries need more rigor. |
| Sprint 5 scaffolding/templates | `led/light` or `thoughtful/light` | Once contracts exist, this is mostly following patterns. Use `thoughtful` only if plan-builder extraction is gnarly. |
| Sprint 6 agent index/legibility | `thoughtful/light` | Product judgment and output shape matter, but blast radius is lower. |
| Sprint 7 rich example + external elements | `premium//high +prep` | Remotion registry/typegen behavior is an integration unknown; prep is justified. |
| Sprint 8 legacy migration | `thoughtful` or `premium//medium` | Depends on which built-in pack is migrated. Use `premium` if it touches core runtime assumptions. |
| Optional Sprint 9 sandbox | `premium/robust/high +prep` | Security boundary plus likely external/container behavior discovery. |

My main recommendation: split **Milestone 1** into at least three megaplan runs, not one: Sprint 0/1 for contract, Sprint 2 for resolver/runtime, Sprint 3 for local install. The first milestone is a product milestone, but it is too much for one megaplan by the rubric’s “two weeks max” rule.

Also, the brief quality matters more than the profile. Before running Sprint 1, tighten the doc by turning the open questions into explicit decisions, especially copy-vs-clone/reference, namespace policy, override policy, and whether `STAGE.md` is mandatory. That likely lets Sprint 1 drop from `super-premium` to `premium` without losing quality.

> DEVELOPER

Does this seem mlike a holistically good plan?

> AGENT

Yes, holistically it looks like a good plan.

The strongest parts are:

- It sequences the hard dependencies correctly: contract -> validation -> resolver/runtime -> local install -> Git install.
- It avoids the biggest trap: making Git install visible before local packs are actually runnable.
- It treats pack manifests as the source of truth and avoids manually authored `provides`, which would drift.
- It separates product milestones from implementation sprints, so you do not accidentally “ship” a pile of partial surfaces.
- It keeps elements later, which is right because Remotion registry/typegen makes them materially harder than executors/orchestrators.
- The megaplan settings are now sane: serious enough for cross-cutting work, but not overcranked by default.

The main thing I would still tighten before implementation is Sprint 0. It should produce explicit decisions, not just discussion. Especially:

- copy vs clone vs reference installed packs
- simple pack ids vs stronger namespacing
- whether built-ins can be overridden
- what exactly counts as “activated”
- what a lock/install record contains
- whether external `STAGE.md` is mandatory
- what “validation is not sandboxing” means in CLI wording

My only structural concern: Sprint 1 may still be too broad if it includes schemas, validation, `packs new`, docs, examples, runtime contract, and legacy compatibility all at once. It is doable, but only if the sprint accepts a deliberately thin first schema. Do not try to perfect every component contract in Sprint 1.

So my recommendation is: keep the roadmap, but make Sprint 0 a crisp decision memo and make Sprint 1’s goal “minimum viable contract that validates and scaffolds” rather than “final schema design.” That keeps momentum without baking in vague foundations.

Sprint 0\n+can revise them if implementation research exposes a serious flaw, but later\n+sprints should not proceed with these questions still open.\n+\n+### Install Mode\n+\n+Installed packs should be copied or cloned into Astrid's managed install root,\n+not referenced in place by default.\n+\n+- Local path installs copy the pack into `~/.astrid/packs/<pack-id>/`.\n+- Git installs clone into the same managed root and pin a concrete commit SHA.\n+- Referencing a source directory in place is a future developer mode, not the v1\n+  default.\n+\n+Reasoning: an installed pack should not change just because a builder edits a\n+working tree. Activation should be explicit, repeatable, and reversible.\n+\n+### Pack Ids And Namespacing\n+\n+Pack ids should remain simple strings in v1, with strict validation and\n+collision rejection.\n+\n+- Valid pack ids should be lowercase ASCII identifiers such as `my_project`.\n+- Executor and orchestrator ids remain `<pack>.<slug>`.\n+- Installed packs cannot collide with built-in or already-installed pack ids.\n+- Public registry or publisher namespacing can be added later as metadata\n+  without changing the local runtime id scheme.\n+\n+Reasoning: stronger public namespacing is useful later, but adding it before a\n+registry exists would add ceremony without solving a current local-install\n+problem.\n+\n+### Overrides\n+\n+Installed packs should not override built-in executors, orchestrators, or\n+elements in v1.\n+\n+- Executor and orchestrator collisions are rejected.\n+- Installed element collisions are rejected.\n+- The existing explicit project-local element override layer can remain special\n+  because it is an intentional editing workflow, not a third-party install\n+  behavior.\n+\n+Reasoning: implicit override semantics create confusing and potentially unsafe\n+tool selection. Overrides should require an explicit project-local action.\n+\n+### Activation Semantics\n+\n+A pack is active only after Astrid has:\n+\n+1. Copied or cloned it into the managed install root.\n+2. Parsed `pack.yaml` with the supported schema version.\n+3. Validated all declared component manifests and referenced files.\n+4. Checked id collisions against built-in and installed packs.\n+5. Written the install record atomically.\n+6. Refreshed discovery so list/search/inspect/run all see the same state.\n+\n+Failed validation leaves no active pack. A partially copied or cloned pack is\n+staging data, not an installed pack.\n+\n+### Install Record\n+\n+Each installed pack should have a machine-readable install record containing at\n+least:\n+\n+- Pack id, name, pack version, schema version, and Astrid compatibility range.\n+- Source type: local path or Git.\n+- Source path or Git URL.\n+- For Git installs: requested ref and resolved commit SHA.\n+- For local installs: original source path and copied content digest where\n+  practical.\n+- Install time and last validation time.\n+- Trust tier.\n+- Manifest digest.\n+- Generated component inventory.\n+- Declared secrets and dependencies summary.\n+- Previous active revision pointer when update/rollback exists.\n+\n+Reasoning: inspect, update, rollback, doctor, and agent-index all need the same\n+source of truth for what is installed and why it is trusted enough to run.\n+\n+### External `STAGE.md`\n+\n+`STAGE.md` should remain mandatory for public external executors and\n+orchestrators in v1.\n+\n+- The structured manifest is authoritative for machine behavior.\n+- `STAGE.md` is the human and agent operational guide.\n+- Elements may rely on manifest metadata and component source unless they need\n+  non-obvious usage instructions.\n+\n+Reasoning: the current Astrid operating model already expects stages to have\n+nearby operational instructions. Removing that requirement before agent-index\n+and inspect output are mature would make external tools harder to use safely.\n+\n+### Validation And Sandboxing\n+\n+CLI wording should state plainly that validation is not sandboxing.\n+\n+- Validation means the pack is well-formed, inspectable, and points at declared\n+  files.\n+- Running an installed pack executes code from that pack.\n+- Secrets are passed only to components that declare them, but declaration is\n+  not a security boundary.\n+- Users should install third-party packs only from sources they trust until\n+  sandbox mode exists.\n+\n+Reasoning: install UX should not imply a safety guarantee that validation cannot\n+provide.\n+\n ## Existing Ticket Mapping\n \n Architecture decisions:\n@@\n ## Open Questions\n \n-- Should installed packs be copied, cloned, or referenced in place?\n-- Should pack ids remain simple strings for v1, or should public packs require\n-  stronger global namespacing?\n-- Should a pack be allowed to override built-in element ids, or must overrides\n-  live in an explicit project-local layer?\n-- How much of element support should ship in v1, given Remotion registry and\n-  compilation assumptions?\n-- Should `STAGE.md` remain mandatory for external components, or should a\n-  manifest `docs` field be enough for simple executors?\n+- How much of element support should ship in v1, given Remotion registry and\n+  compilation assumptions?\n*** End Patch\n"
}

> DEVELOPER

ANy other open questions?

> AGENT

Yes. The doc now has one explicit open question, but I’d track a few more as **implementation-level open questions**, not architecture blockers:

1. **Element v1 scope**
   How much external element support ships in v1: validate-only, install/list/inspect, or full registry/typegen/render support?

2. **Local install copy details**
   For `packs install <local-path>`, do we copy all files, respect `.gitignore`, or use an Astrid-specific ignore file like `.astridignore`?

3. **Pack update semantics for local installs**
   Does `packs update <id>` recopy from the original source path, or are local installs immutable until reinstall?

4. **Install record location**
   Is the install record inside `~/.astrid/packs/<pack-id>/`, beside it, or in a central lock/index file?

5. **Validation strictness levels**
   Is there only one `packs validate`, or do we eventually support `--strict` / `--warnings-as-errors`?

6. **Runtime environment boundary**
   For v1, do external pack `run.py` files execute in-process, as subprocesses, or through the same runner path existing executors use?

7. **Schema technology**
   JSON Schema, Pydantic models, or both? This matters for error quality and docs generation.

8. **Pack examples and test assets**
   Are examples allowed to include small media/test fixtures, and what size/type limits keep packs sane?

None of these overturn the plan. The only one I’d resolve before Sprint 1 starts is **schema technology**. The others can be decided inside the sprint where they first matter.

  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: docs/git-backed-packs-plan.md\n@@\n ### Install Mode\n \n Installed packs should be copied or cloned into Astrid's managed install root,\n not referenced in place by default.\n \n - Local path installs copy the pack into `~/.astrid/packs/<pack-id>/`.\n - Git installs clone into the same managed root and pin a concrete commit SHA.\n+- Local path copies respect the source repository's `.gitignore`.\n - Referencing a source directory in place is a future developer mode, not the v1\n   default.\n \n Reasoning: an installed pack should not change just because a builder edits a\n-working tree. Recopy-on-update preserves snapshot\n+semantics while avoiding silent mutation.\n@@\n ### Install Record\n \n Each installed pack should have a machine-readable install record containing at\n least:\n@@\n - Previous active revision pointer when update/rollback exists.\n \n+The authoritative install record should live at:\n+\n+```text\n+~/.astrid/packs/<pack-id>/.astrid/install.json\n+```\n+\n+A central index may be generated later for faster list/search, but it should be\n+a cache rebuilt from per-pack install records rather than the only source of\n+truth.\n+\n Reasoning: inspect, update, rollback, doctor, and agent-index all need the same\n source of truth for what is installed and why it is trusted enough to run.\n@@\n ### Validation And Sandboxing\n \n CLI wording should state plainly that validation is not sandboxing.\n@@\n Reasoning: install UX should not imply a safety guarantee that validation cannot\n provide.\n+\n+### Validation Strictness\n+\n+`packs validate` should be strict by default about correctness errors and\n+non-fatal about warnings.\n+\n+- Errors fail validation and block install.\n+- Warnings are shown but do not fail validation by default.\n+- `--warnings-as-errors` should be available for CI and curated-pack checks.\n+- A separate `--strict` mode is not needed in v1 unless a concrete second tier\n+  of checks emerges.\n+\n+Reasoning: one normal validation mode keeps builder UX simple. Warnings still\n+leave room for advisory checks such as docs quality, optional examples, or future\n+best practices.\n+\n+### Runtime Boundary\n+\n+External executor and orchestrator `run.py` files should execute through the\n+same runner path as existing folder-backed tools, using the manifest runtime\n+adapter rather than importing arbitrary modules directly from discovery code.\n+\n+- The manifest declares the runtime adapter, entrypoint file, and callable or\n+  command shape.\n+- Runtime execution receives argv/env according to the documented adapter\n+  contract.\n+- Validation may import or load enough to verify the entrypoint, but normal\n+  execution should happen through the runner path.\n+\n+Reasoning: external packs should not introduce a second execution model. The\n+runner path is where output conventions, errors, and future sandbox wrapping\n+belong.\n+\n+### Schema Technology\n+\n+The published pack and component contracts should be JSON Schema documents,\n+validated with `jsonschema`.\n+\n+- Author-facing manifests remain YAML or JSON.\n+- YAML should be parsed with PyYAML `safe_load`; PyYAML should become an explicit\n+  dependency instead of relying on indirect installs.\n+- JSON Schema is the published contract for docs, validation, editor tooling,\n+  and cross-language consumers.\n+- Python dataclasses or typed helpers may wrap validated data internally, but\n+  they should not replace JSON Schema as the external contract.\n+\n+Reasoning: Astrid already depends on `jsonschema`, and JSON Schema gives a\n+language-neutral contract for agents, docs, editors, and future non-Python pack\n+tooling.\n+\n+### Examples And Test Assets\n+\n+Example packs may include small fixtures and media assets when they are needed\n+to prove behavior.\n+\n+- Keep example assets small and source-controlled only when they are genuinely\n+  part of the example.\n+- Generated outputs, rendered videos, caches, model weights, virtualenvs, and\n+  downloaded media stay out of packs.\n+- Larger examples should fetch or generate their heavy inputs during the run, or\n+  document where the user should place them.\n+- Validation should warn when example assets look unusually large, but size\n+  limits should not block v1 unless they are causing real repo bloat.\n+\n+Reasoning: media packs need realistic examples, but installed packs should not\n+become a dumping ground for generated outputs or heavyweight local state.\n+\n+### Element V1 Scope\n+\n+External element support should ship all the way through in v1, not as\n+validate-only metadata.\n+\n+- External element manifests validate.\n+- Installed external elements appear in list/search/inspect output.\n+- Registry generation and type generation include installed external elements.\n+- A rendered timeline can use an installed external element.\n+- Element failures have a clear debug path through validation, registry\n+  generation, type generation, and render.\n+\n+Reasoning: elements are part of the core pack promise. > DEVELOPER

Any abstractons here that are overly complex?

> AGENT

Yes. A few abstractions are at risk of getting too heavy if implemented too early.

The main ones I’d watch:

1. **`PackResolver`**
Good abstraction, but keep it concrete. It should answer “where is this pack/component/file?” and “what revision is installed?” Do not let it become a plugin framework, dependency resolver, policy engine, or runtime supervisor.

2. **Trust tiers**
Useful language, but v1 should probably only implement labels and warnings:
`built-in`, `local`, `git`.
Avoid a deep trust-policy system until sandboxing, signatures, or a registry exists.

3. **Generated component inventory**
Good idea, but make it an install/inspect artifact, not a second registry format people have to reason about. The source of truth should stay `pack.yaml` + component manifests.

4. **Install record**
Needed, but keep it one JSON file first. Avoid central database/index/revision store unless performance or rollback actually forces it. A generated cache is fine later.

5. **Schema system**
JSON Schema is right, but don’t create both JSON Schema and a parallel Pydantic model hierarchy that can drift. If Python types exist, they should be thin wrappers around already-validated data.

6. **Agent index**
Do not overbuild summarization in v1. Start with deterministic assembly from manifest fields and docs paths. Avoid LLM-generated summaries or heuristic doc digestion until the structured fields prove insufficient.

7. **Runtime adapter model**
For v1, only support `python-cli`. Design the manifest so more adapters can come later, but don’t build a generic adapter framework now.

8. **External elements**
This is the most complex part. The product target can be “all the way,” but implementation should still isolate it into a later sprint. Don’t let element support distort the executor/orchestrator contract.

The one abstraction I would keep firmly is **manifest-first packs with a resolver**. That is the backbone. The risky complexity is anything that turns v1 into a general plugin marketplace, policy engine, or package manager before local runnable packs exist.

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

I added an **Abstraction Levels** section to [docs/git-backed-packs-plan.md](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/Astrid/docs/git-backed-packs-plan.md:1049).

The core decisions are:

- Keep `PackResolver`, but only as a concrete resource locator.
- Trust tiers are labels/warnings, not a policy engine.
- Generated inventory is an artifact, not a second authored registry.
- Install state is one per-pack JSON file, not a database.
- JSON Schema is the external contract; Python types are internal wrappers only.
- Agent index is deterministic assembly, not LLM summarization.
- Runtime adapters start with one external adapter: `python-cli`.
- External elements get a focused Remotion bridge, not a general frontend asset system.
- Dependencies stay declarative/diagnostic.
- Local installs are snapshots; updates explicitly recopy and revalidate.

The litmus test I added: keep an abstraction if removing it would reintroduce duplicated discovery/runtime behavior; defer it if it mainly supports a future marketplace, registry, sandbox, or package manager.

> DEVELOPER

Have you made deicisions?

> AGENT

Yes. The plan now makes decisions, not just lists options.

The decided points are:

- **Install mode:** local installs copy into `~/.astrid/packs/<pack-id>/`; Git installs clone there and pin a commit.
- **Ignore behavior:** local copies respect `.gitignore`.
- **Local updates:** `packs update` recopies from the original source path, validates, then activates.
- **Pack ids:** simple lowercase ids for v1; collisions are rejected.
- **Overrides:** installed packs cannot override built-ins in v1.
- **Activation:** a pack is active only after copy/clone, parse, validate, collision check, atomic install record write, and discovery refresh.
- **Install record:** per-pack `.astrid/install.json`; central indexes can only be generated caches later.
- **`STAGE.md`:** mandatory for external executors/orchestrators in v1.
- **Validation:** errors block install, warnings do not; `--warnings-as-errors` for CI.
- **Sandboxing:** validation is explicitly not sandboxing.
- **Runtime:** external tools execute through the existing runner path via one v1 adapter: `python-cli`.
- **Schema tech:** JSON Schema as the published contract, YAML parsed with PyYAML `safe_load`.
- **Examples:** small real fixtures allowed; generated/heavy assets excluded.
- **Elements:** v1 target is full external element support, including registry/typegen/render, but later in the roadmap.
- **Abstraction levels:** each risky abstraction has a v1 boundary and an explicit “do not build yet” line.

So Sprint 0 is now less “decide everything” and more “confirm or revise these defaults before implementation.”

> AGENT

Codex sense-check completed. It agrees the plan is coherent, but flagged several things worth tightening before implementation.

I agree with these:

- **V1 threat model needs to be explicit.** Installed packs are trusted executable code; validation is not sandboxing. The doc says this, but should define exactly what trust summary/confirmation/secrets/env inheritance mean.
- **Runtime contract needs to be promoted.** `python-cli` should have a real contract in the manifest section: argv, env, cwd, outputs, exit codes, callable/command behavior.
- **Install/update atomicity needs more detail.** Staging directory, active pointer, install lock, concurrent update behavior, rollback scope, broken-record recovery.
- **Split `PackResolver` from install state.** Resolver should stay read-only and deterministic. An `InstalledPackStore` or similar should own install records/revisions.
- **Component scaffolding should move earlier.** Minimal `executors new` / `orchestrators new` probably belongs before Sprint 5, because otherwise Milestone 1 can validate packs but not easily create runnable ones.
- **Element identity needs a decision.** Bare element IDs are collision-prone once external packs exist. Decide pack-qualified vs alias vs reject-collisions before element work.
- **Validation must not import arbitrary Git code by default.** Static validation first; runtime smoke test should be explicit/trusted.

I mostly reject or soften this one:

- **Namespace concern:** Codex is right that public Git packs collide, but I still think simple local IDs + collision rejection is fine for v1. I’d add “source identity” metadata separately from CLI ID, rather than making users type globally namespaced IDs immediately.

Concrete doc changes I’d make next:

1. Add **V1 Non-Negotiable Decisions** covering threat model, runtime contract, atomic install, namespace/source identity, resolver/store split.
2. Replace “runtime entrypoints import or load successfully” with “static path/schema validation; optional trusted runtime smoke test.”
3. Add `InstalledPackStore` to abstraction levels.
4. Move minimal executor/orchestrator scaffolding into Sprint 1 or 2.
5. Add an element ID policy decision before Sprint 7.

Prefer fields like:\n \n ```yaml\n runtime:\n   type: python-cli\n   entrypoint: run.py\n   callable: main\n ```\n \n over internal names such as `runtime_module`.\n+\n+For v1, `python-cli` is the only external runtime adapter. Its contract should\n+be explicit before implementation:\n+\n+- The `entrypoint` path is resolved relative to the component root.\n+- The entrypoint is invoked through Astrid's normal runner path, not by discovery\n+  code importing arbitrary pack modules.\n+- The working directory is the component root unless the runner already has a\n+  stronger convention.\n+- CLI arguments are passed exactly as the user supplied them after `--`, plus\n+  any runner-owned arguments such as `--out` that the manifest declares.\n+- Environment variables are inherited from the Astrid process except secrets:\n+  declared secrets are the only secrets Astrid intentionally surfaces in install\n+  summaries and future secret-passing UX.\n+- Exit code `0` means success. Non-zero exits are wrapped with the component id,\n+  command, and relevant stderr/stdout excerpts.\n+- Outputs are declared in the manifest or stage docs; v1 validation checks the\n+  declarations but does not enforce every output at runtime unless existing\n+  executor/orchestrator runners already do.\n \n ## Agent-Facing Index\n@@\n - Pack id and component ids are valid and consistently namespaced.\n - Declared content roots exist.\n - Referenced docs, schemas, runtime files, and examples exist.\n-- Runtime entrypoints import or load successfully.\n+- Runtime entrypoint paths exist and are statically well-formed.\n - No undeclared manifests are accidentally exposed.\n - No duplicate ids collide with installed or built-in packs.\n - Required secret declarations are well formed.\n@@\n The current custom flat YAML parser should be removed or bypassed for manifests\n that users author.\n+\n+Validation must not execute arbitrary pack code by default. A separate trusted\n+runtime smoke test may import or execute an entrypoint after the user confirms\n+they trust the pack, but normal schema/path validation should remain static.\n@@\n-Introduce a `PackResolver` abstraction as part of this work. It should resolve\n-pack roots, component roots, docs, schemas, runtime files, examples, assets, and\n-installed revisions. Runtime resolution, validation, inspect, and agent-index\n-should consume this resolver instead of assuming components live under the\n-source tree.\n+Introduce a `PackResolver` abstraction as part of this work. It should be\n+read-only and deterministic: resolve pack roots, component roots, docs, schemas,\n+runtime files, examples, and assets from already-known pack roots. Runtime\n+resolution, validation, inspect, and agent-index should consume this resolver\n+instead of assuming components live under the source tree.\n+\n+Install records, active revisions, rollback pointers, and install/update locks\n+belong to a separate `InstalledPackStore`, not to `PackResolver`.\n*** End Patch\n"
}

{
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: docs/git-backed-packs-plan.md\n@@\n ## Initial Architecture Decisions\n \n These decisions should be treated as the starting point for Sprint 0. Sprint 0\n can revise them if implementation research exposes a serious flaw, but later\n sprints should not proceed with these questions still open.\n \n+### V1 Threat Model\n+\n+Installed packs are trusted executable code.\n+\n+- Validation proves structure and declared references, not safety.\n+- `packs install` for local paths and Git URLs should show a trust summary\n+  before activation unless `--yes` is passed.\n+- The trust summary should include source, resolved revision or source path,\n+  pack id, component inventory, declared runtime entrypoints, declared secrets,\n+  dependency declarations, docs paths, and warnings.\n+- Pack subprocesses inherit the normal Astrid process environment in v1.\n+- Astrid should only intentionally surface and summarize secrets declared by the\n+  pack, but declaration is not a sandbox or permission boundary.\n+- Users should treat third-party Git packs like scripts they chose to run.\n+\n+Reasoning: v1 can make installed code explicit and auditable, but it should not\n+claim isolation until sandbox mode exists.\n+\n ### Install Mode\n \n Installed packs should be copied or cloned into Astrid's managed install root,\n not referenced in place by default.\n@@\n ### Pack Ids And Namespacing\n \n Pack ids should remain simple strings in v1, with strict validation and\n collision rejection.\n \n - Valid pack ids should be lowercase ASCII identifiers such as `my_project`.\n - Executor and orchestrator ids remain `<pack>.<slug>`.\n - Installed packs cannot collide with built-in or already-installed pack ids.\n+- Git URL, requested ref, resolved commit SHA, and local source path are source\n+  identity metadata in the install record, not the CLI id.\n - Public registry or publisher namespacing can be added later as metadata\n   without changing the local runtime id scheme.\n \n Reasoning: stronger public namespacing is useful later, but adding it before a\n registry exists would add ceremony without solving a current local-install\n problem.\n+\n+### Element Ids\n+\n+Installed external element ids should remain bare within their element kind for\n+v1, but collisions are rejected.\n+\n+- Timeline references keep the existing `{kind, id}` shape.\n+- Installed element manifests also carry `pack_id`.\n+- An installed element cannot collide with a built-in element or another\n+  installed element of the same kind.\n+- Pack-qualified element references or aliases can be added later if a public\n+  ecosystem needs multiple same-named elements active at once.\n+\n+Reasoning: this preserves current timeline compatibility while avoiding implicit\n+override behavior. The cost is that users cannot install two packs that provide\n+the same element id until a future aliasing model exists.\n@@\n ### Activation Semantics\n \n A pack is active only after Astrid has:\n \n-1. Copied or cloned it into the managed install root.\n+1. Acquired an install/update lock for the target pack id.\n+2. Copied or cloned it into a staging directory under the managed install root.\n-2. Parsed `pack.yaml` with the supported schema version.\n-3. Validated all declared component manifests and referenced files.\n-4. Checked id collisions against built-in and installed packs.\n-5. Written the install record atomically.\n-6. Refreshed discovery so list/search/inspect/run all see the same state.\n+3. Parsed `pack.yaml` with the supported schema version.\n+4. Validated all declared component manifests and referenced files.\n+5. Checked id collisions against built-in and installed packs.\n+6. Written the staged install record.\n+7. Atomically moved the staging directory into the revision store.\n+8. Atomically updated the active pointer for the pack id.\n+9. Refreshed discovery so list/search/inspect/run all see the same state.\n \n Failed validation leaves no active pack. A partially copied or cloned pack is\n staging data, not an installed pack.\n+\n+The managed install root should separate revisions from the active pointer, for\n+example:\n+\n+```text\n+~/.astrid/packs/\n+  my_project/\n+    active -> revisions/<revision-id>/\n+    revisions/\n+      <revision-id>/\n+        pack.yaml\n+        .astrid/install.json\n+```\n+\n+Read commands use the active pointer. Update and uninstall commands take the\n+install lock. If another process is already running a component from the old\n+active path, v1 does not have to interrupt it; the new active revision applies\n+to subsequent discovery and runs.\n@@\n-The authoritative install record should live at:\n+The authoritative install record for each revision should live at:\n \n ```text\n-~/.astrid/packs/<pack-id>/.astrid/install.json\n+~/.astrid/packs/<pack-id>/revisions/<revision-id>/.astrid/install.json\n ```\n*** End Patch\n"
}
